## 2.2 Privacy Leakage from Model Updates

MIAs against Model Updates. Membership inference attack (MIA) is one of the most common <sub>p</sub>rivac<sub>y</sub> attacks a<sub>g</sub>ainst machine learnin<sub>g</sub> models [47]. It aims to determine whether a s<sub>p</sub>ecific data instance was included in the trainin<sub>g</sub> set of a tar<sub>g</sub>et model [49, 52]. Ja<sub>g</sub>ielski et al. [26] develo<sub>p</sub>ed the first MIA to attack models with u<sub>p</sub>dates. Th<sub>ey cons</sub>id<sub>ere</sub>d b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e-up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e an</sub>d <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e-up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e se</sub>tti<sub>ngs</sub> and developed two attacks: (1) Membership inference, where the <sub>a</sub>d<sub>versary a</sub>i<sub>ms</sub> t<sub>o</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>er w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er a spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d in the update set at a particular time point; and (2) Entry inference, which aims to infer which time epoch the data sample enters the dataset. T<sup>h</sup>ey <sup>d</sup>erived the o<sub>p</sub>timal SeMI attack as a likelihood-ratio (LR) test, and d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gne</sub>d <sub>a</sub> LR<sub>-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>w</sub>hit<sub>e-</sub>b<sub>ox a</sub>tt<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a</sub> l<sub>oss-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d bl<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>-</sub>b<sub>ox</sub> attack. To capture the temporal <sub>mem</sub>b<sub>ers</sub>hi<sub>p</sub> <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>vacy</sub> <sub>a</sub>t b<sub>o</sub>th ti<sub>mes</sub>t<sub>amp</sub> l<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>s,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>use</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>un</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d measurement framework that evaluates the precision and recall of th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erre</sub>d <sub>mem</sub>b<sub>ers</sub>hi<sub>p</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> F<sub>orma</sub>ll<sub>y,</sub> l<sub>e</sub>t ${ \cal { T } } ^ { * } ( x )$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>ru</sub>th t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>mem</sub>b<sub>ers</sub>hi<sub>p</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>e</sub> <sub>�,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>e</sub>t $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { T } } ( \boldsymbol { x } )$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f t<sub>empora</sub>l i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erre</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> adversar<sub>y</sub>. We define timestamplevel precision (T-Pre) as the fraction of the inferred timestamps that are correct (i.e., the<sub>y</sub> belon<sub>g</sub> to the <sub>g</sub>round-truth set), and timestamp-level recall (T-Rec) as the fraction of ground-truth ti<sub>mes</sub>t<sub>amps</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>are correc</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erre</sub>d<sub>:</sub>

Example 2. ## 4.2 Straw-man Approaches and Their Insuficiency

## 5.3 Constructing Membership Intervals

A major <sup>l</sup>imitation o<sup>f</sup> t<sup>h</sup>e sequence <sup>l</sup>a<sup>b</sup>e<sup>l</sup>ing mo<sup>d</sup>e<sup>l</sup> is t<sup>h</sup>at assigning l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l ti<sub>mes</sub>t<sub>amps</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o cap</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>l d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>enc</sub>i<sub>es</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> <sub>en</sub>t<sub>ry</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ex</sub>it <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l ma<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redict an exit <sub>p</sub>oint before the corres<sub>p</sub>ondin<sub>g</sub> entr<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>oint<sub>,</sub> or <sub>g</sub>enerate isolated entr<sub>y</sub> or exit labels in the se<sub>q</sub>uence. Such inconsistencies can <sub>p</sub>revent the construction of valid membershi<sub>p</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>

First, we formally define valid membership intervals.

Definition 4 (Valid Membership Intervals). Let $T = \left( ( t _ { 1 } , y _ { 1 } ) \right.$ $( t _ { 2 } , y _ { 2 } ) , \dots , ( t _ { N } , y _ { N } ) )$ be a sequence of timestamps, where each timestamp $t _ { i }$ is assigned a label $y _ { i } \in \{ \mathsf { N } , \mathsf { E } , \mathsf { X } \}$ by our BiLSTM model, representing a non-change, entry, and exit point, respectively. Let $E ( T ) = \{ i \mid y _ { i } = \mathsf E \}$ , and $X ( T ) = \{ i \mid y _ { i } = X \}$ denote the sets ofentry and exit timestamps, respectively. A set of membership intervals is valid ifthere exists an injective matching function: $\phi : X ( T )  E ( T )$ that satisfies the following three requirements:

(1) Correct ordering. Every exit point is matched to a preceding entry point: $\forall j \in X ( T ) , \phi ( j ) < j$

(2) Entry-exit matching. Every exit point in $X ( T )$ is matched to exactly one entry point in �(� ), while each entry point �(�) is matched to at most one exit point in �(�). Consequently, an entry point may remain unmatched, corresponding to a membership interval that extends to the last observed timestamp $t _ { N }$

(3) Non-overlapping intervals. For any two intervals $I _ { i } , I _ { j }$ induced by �, $I _ { i } \cap I _ { j } = \varnothing$

To construct valid tem<sub>p</sub>oral membershi<sub>p</sub> intervals<sub>,</sub> we desi<sub>g</sub>n a three-ste<sub>p</sub> <sub>p</sub>rocedure: Ste<sub>p</sub> 1 extracts the entr<sub>y</sub> and exit <sub>p</sub>oints and <sub>p</sub>artitions them into <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s<sub>,</sub> Ste<sub>p</sub> 2 selects a re<sub>p</sub>resentative timestam<sub>p</sub> for each <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p,</sub> and Ste<sub>p</sub> 3 <sub>p</sub>airs the re<sub>p</sub>resentatives to form membershi<sub>p</sub> intervals. Ste<sub>p</sub> 1 ensures Re<sub>q</sub>uirement 1<sub>,</sub> while Ste<sub>p</sub>s 2 & 3 ensures Re<sub>q</sub>uirements 2 & 3. of valid membershi<sub>p</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub> N<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>, we</sub> di<sub>scuss</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>s o</sub>f <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ep.</sub>

## 6.3 Robustness to Shadow Model Heterogeneity

I<sub>n prac</sub>ti<sub>ce, an a</sub>d<sub>versary</sub>’<sub>s</sub> k<sub>now</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge o</sub>f th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>d<sub>ow</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>, mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure, an</sub>d <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e a</sub>l<sub>gor</sub>ith<sub>m may no</sub>t <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gn w</sub>ith th<sub>ose use</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>.</sub> I<sub>n</sub> thi<sub>s sec</sub>ti<sub>on, we con</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>t <sub>a compre</sub>h<sub>ens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> set of experiments evaluating TMIA’s performance across varying <sub>m</sub>i<sub>sma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>scenar</sub>i<sub>os,</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>di<sub>ng m</sub>i<sub>sma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s, m</sub>i<sub>sma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure, an</sub>d <sub>m</sub>i<sub>sma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e a</sub>l<sub>gor</sub>ith<sub>ms.</sub> D<sub>ue</sub> t<sub>o</sub> s<sub>p</sub>ace limit<sub>,</sub> we onl<sub>y</sub> re<sub>p</sub>ort the T-F1 results for the three LMs in this section. The I-F1 <sub>p</sub>erformance can be found in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.

Mismatched Datasets. We fix the target model to Pythia-70M and var<sub>y</sub> the shadow and tar<sub>g</sub>et datasets amon<sub>g</sub> SST5<sub>,</sub> News20<sub>,</sub> and MNLI. Figure 5a reports the T-F1 of TMIA under these settings. As expected, TMIA achieves the highest T-F1 when the shadow and t<sub>arge</sub>t d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s co</sub>i<sub>nc</sub>id<sub>e.</sub> N<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>y, even w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>d<sub>ow mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l is trained on a diferent dataset, TMIA remains highly efective, exhibitin<sub>g</sub> a mar<sub>g</sub>inal reduction in T-F1 of at most 4.95% across all sett<sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub>s.

Mismatched Model Architectures. We fix SST5 as the target d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>vary</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>d<sub>ow an</sub>d t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s across</sub> th<sub>ree arc</sub>hi<sub>-</sub> t<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ures:</sub> P<sub>y</sub>thi<sub>a-</sub>70M<sub>,</sub> T5<sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d GPT<sub>-</sub>2<sub>.</sub> Fi<sub>gure</sub> 5b <sub>repor</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> T<sub>-</sub>F1 results. In all cases, TMIA achieves its best performance when th<sub>e s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>d<sub>ow an</sub>d t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s s</sub>h<sub>are</sub> th<sub>e same arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure.</sub> N<sub>ever-</sub> theless, TMIA remains robust when the two models use diferent <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ures, w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> T<sub>-</sub>F1 d<sub>egra</sub>d<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f <sub>no more</sub> th<sub>an</sub> 2<sub>.</sub>1% <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>es</sub>t<sub>-per</sub>f<sub>orm</sub>i<sub>ng s</sub>h<sub>a</sub>d<sub>ow arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure.</sub>

P<sub>roof.</sub> Fi<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>, we</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> l<sub>emma</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>presen</sub>t<sub>s an</sub> <sup>im</sup>p<sup>ortant</sup> p<sup>ro</sup>p<sup>ert</sup>y <sup>of</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>re</sup>p<sup>resentative</sup> <sup>timestam</sup>p<sup>s</sup> <sup>out</sup>p<sup>ut</sup> <sup>b</sup>y <sup>S</sup>te<sub>p</sub> 2.

Lemma 1 (Alternating Labels of Consecutive Representative Timestamps). Let $\tau = \{ \tau _ { 1 } , . . . , \tau _ { K } \}$ denote the set ofrepresentative timestamps. Then no two consecutive timestamps in � share the same label; that is, for all $i = 1 , \ldots , K - 1$ , we have

$$
\ell _ { \tau _ { i } } \neq \ell _ { \tau _ { i + 1 } } .
$$

If $\ell _ { \tau _ { i } } = \ell _ { \tau _ { i + 1 } }$ <sub>,</sub> St<sub>ep</sub> 1 <sub>wou</sub>ld mer<sub>g</sub>e the <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s of these two re<sub>p</sub>resentative timestam<sub>p</sub>s into a sin<sub>g</sub>le lar<sub>g</sub>er <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>. The re<sub>p</sub>resentative timestam<sub>p</sub> of the mer<sub>g</sub>ed <sub>group</sub> <sub>wou</sub>ld th<sub>en</sub> b<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>one</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>er</sub> <sub>con</sub>fid<sub>ence</sub> <sub>score</sub> <sup>amon</sup>g $\ell _ { \tau _ { i } }$ <sub>an</sub>d $\ell _ { \tau _ { i + 1 } }$ □

## B.5 Update Methods

W<sub>e</sub> <sub>cons</sub>id<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>paper:</sub>

• Fine-tuning (FT): For removal, we fine-tune on forget sam-<sub>p</sub>l<sub>es</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> i<sub>ncorrec</sub>t l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s;</sub> f<sub>or</sub> i<sub>nser</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>use</sub> <sub>new</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>dd<sub>e</sub>d <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>es</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>correc</sub>t l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>

• Gradient Ascent (GA): GA [27] increases the cross-entropy l<sub>oss</sub> <sub>on</sub> f<sub>orge</sub>t <sub>samp</sub>l<sub>es,</sub> <sub>com</sub>bi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n-se</sub>t l<sub>oss</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>p</sub>reserve utilit<sub>y</sub>.
